</details>


### [238] [Maximum Entropy Behavior Exploration for Sim2Real Zero-Shot Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.25464)
*Jiajun Hu,Nuria Armengol Urpi,Jin Cheng,Stelian Coros*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FB-MEBE: Online zero-shot RL algorithm combining unsupervised behavior exploration with regularization critic for quadrupedal control, enabling direct hardware deployment without finetuning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Zero-shot RL requires diverse pretraining datasets, but undirected exploration yields low-diversity data leading to poor downstream performance and impractical hardware deployment. Need for online zero-shot RL with better exploration for real robotic systems.

Method: FB-MEBE combines unsupervised behavior exploration (maximizing entropy of achieved behavior distribution) with regularization critic to shape policies toward natural, physically plausible behaviors. Builds upon Forward-Backward algorithm.

Result: FB-MEBE achieves improved performance compared to other exploration strategies in simulated downstream tasks. Produces natural policies that can be deployed directly to hardware without further finetuning.

Conclusion: FB-MEBE enables effective online zero-shot RL for quadrupedal control by addressing exploration diversity and policy naturalness, facilitating direct hardware deployment.

Abstract: Zero-shot reinforcement learning (RL) algorithms aim to learn a family of policies from a reward-free dataset, and recover optimal policies for any reward function directly at test time. Naturally, the quality of the pretraining dataset determines the performance of the recovered policies across tasks. However, pre-collecting a relevant, diverse dataset without prior knowledge of the downstream tasks of interest remains a challenge. In this work, we study $\textit{online}$ zero-shot RL for quadrupedal control on real robotic systems, building upon the Forward-Backward (FB) algorithm. We observe that undirected exploration yields low-diversity data, leading to poor downstream performance and rendering policies impractical for direct hardware deployment. Therefore, we introduce FB-MEBE, an online zero-shot RL algorithm that combines an unsupervised behavior exploration strategy with a regularization critic. FB-MEBE promotes exploration by maximizing the entropy of the achieved behavior distribution. Additionally, a regularization critic shapes the recovered policies toward more natural and physically plausible behaviors. We empirically demonstrate that FB-MEBE achieves and improved performance compared to other exploration strategies in a range of simulated downstream tasks, and that it renders natural policies that can be seamlessly deployed to hardware without further finetuning. Videos and code available on our website.

</details>


### [239] [Not a fragment, but the whole: Map-based evaluation of data-driven Fire Danger Index models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.25469)
*Shahbaz Alvi,Italo Epicoco,Jose Maria Costa Saura*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes new evaluation method for wildfire forecasting models that accounts for operational decision-making needs, particularly false positive rates, and shows ensemble ML improves fire identification while reducing false alarms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard ML evaluation metrics provide limited measure of operational performance for Fire Danger Index forecasting, and current evaluations inadequately account for false positive rates despite their critical importance in real-world fire management decisions.

Method: Revisits daily FDI model evaluation paradigm and proposes novel evaluation method aligned with real-world decision-making; systematically assesses performance in predicting fire activity and false positives; demonstrates ensemble ML approach.

Result: Ensemble of ML models improves both fire identification accuracy and reduces false positives (false alarms) compared to individual models.

Conclusion: New evaluation framework better aligns wildfire forecasting model assessment with operational needs, and ensemble ML methods offer superior performance for practical fire danger prediction by balancing accurate fire identification with reduced false alarms.

Abstract: A growing body of literature has focused on predicting wildfire occurrence using machine learning methods, capitalizing on high-resolution data and fire predictors that canonical process-based frameworks largely ignore. Standard evaluation metrics for an ML classifier, while important, provide a potentially limited measure of the model's operational performance for the Fire Danger Index (FDI) forecast. Furthermore, model evaluation is frequently conducted without adequately accounting for false positive rates, despite their critical relevance in operational contexts. In this paper, we revisit the daily FDI model evaluation paradigm and propose a novel method for evaluating a forest fire forecasting model that is aligned with real-world decision-making. Furthermore, we systematically assess performance in accurately predicting fire activity and the false positives (false alarms). We further demonstrate that an ensemble of ML models improves both fire identification and reduces false positives.

</details>


### [240] [Causal-INSIGHT: Probing Temporal Models to Extract Causal Structure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.25473)
*Benjamin Redden,Hui Wang,Shuyan Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Causal-INSIGHT is a model-agnostic framework that extracts directed temporal influence structure from trained predictors by analyzing their responses to intervention-inspired input perturbations, using a sparsity-aware graph selection criterion called Qbic.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understanding directed temporal interactions in multivariate time series is crucial for interpreting complex dynamical systems and the predictive models trained on them. Current methods often focus on inferring causal structure at the data-generating process level, but there's a need for approaches that reveal how trained predictors actually use temporal dependencies for making predictions.

Method: Causal-INSIGHT is a post-hoc interpretation framework that analyzes fixed, pre-trained predictors by applying systematic, intervention-inspired input clamping at inference time. From these responses, it constructs directed temporal influence signals. The method introduces Qbic, a sparsity-aware graph selection criterion that balances predictive fidelity and structural complexity without requiring ground-truth graph labels.

Result: Experiments across synthetic, simulated, and realistic benchmarks show that Causal-INSIGHT generalizes across diverse backbone architectures, maintains competitive structural accuracy, and yields significant improvements in temporal delay localization when applied to existing predictors.

Conclusion: Causal-INSIGHT provides an effective framework for extracting model-implied directed temporal influence structure from trained predictors, offering insights into how these models leverage temporal dependencies for prediction without requiring ground-truth causal labels.

Abstract: Understanding directed temporal interactions in multivariate time series is essential for interpreting complex dynamical systems and the predictive models trained on them. We present Causal-INSIGHT, a model-agnostic, post-hoc interpretation framework for extracting model-implied (predictor-dependent), directed, time-lagged influence structure from trained temporal predictors. Rather than inferring causal structure at the level of the data-generating process, Causal-INSIGHT analyzes how a fixed, pre-trained predictor responds to systematic, intervention-inspired input clamping applied at inference time.
  From these responses, we construct directed temporal influence signals that reflect the dependencies the predictor relies on for prediction, and introduce Qbic, a sparsity-aware graph selection criterion that balances predictive fidelity and structural complexity without requiring ground-truth graph labels. Experiments across synthetic, simulated, and realistic benchmarks show that Causal-INSIGHT generalizes across diverse backbone architectures, maintains competitive structural accuracy, and yields significant improvements in temporal delay localization when applied to existing predictors.

</details>


### [241] [How Class Ontology and Data Scale Affect Audio Transfer Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.25476)
*Manuel Milling,Andreas Triantafyllopoulos,Alexander Gebhard,Simon Rampp,Björn W. Schuller*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Audio-to-audio transfer learning study shows that while increasing pre-training data size helps, task similarity between pre-training and downstream tasks has greater impact on transfer performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Despite widespread use of transfer learning in deep learning, there are still open questions about when and how well it works, particularly regarding understanding the inner workings and factors affecting transfer learning performance.

Method: Rigorous study of audio-to-audio transfer learning: pre-train various models on ontology-based subsets of AudioSet, then fine-tune them on three computer audition tasks (acoustic scene recognition, bird activity recognition, and speech command recognition).

Result: Both increasing the number of samples and classes in pre-training data have positive impact on transfer learning, but this effect is generally surpassed by similarity between pre-training and downstream tasks, which enables models to learn comparable features.

Conclusion: Task similarity between pre-training and downstream tasks is a more critical factor than simply increasing pre-training data volume for successful transfer learning in audio domains.

Abstract: Transfer learning is a crucial concept within deep learning that allows artificial neural networks to benefit from a large pre-training data basis when confronted with a task of limited data. Despite its ubiquitous use and clear benefits, there are still many open questions regarding the inner workings of transfer learning and, in particular, regarding the understanding of when and how well it works. To that extent, we perform a rigorous study focusing on audio-to-audio transfer learning, in which we pre-train various model states on (ontology-based) subsets of AudioSet and fine-tune them on three computer audition tasks, namely acoustic scene recognition, bird activity recognition, and speech command recognition. We report that increasing the number of samples and classes in the pre-training data both have a positive impact on transfer learning. This is, however, generally surpassed by similarity between pre-training and the downstream task, which can lead the model to learn comparable features.

</details>


### [242] [Interpretable PM2.5 Forecasting for Urban Air Quality: A Comparative Study of Operational Time-Series Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.25495)
*Moazzam Umer Gondal,Hamad ul Qudous,Asma Ahmad Farhan,Sultan Alamri*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Lightweight interpretable models (SARIMAX, Facebook Prophet, NeuralProphet) with leakage-aware workflow and adaptive regimes achieve competitive PM2.5 forecasting in Beijing, with Facebook Prophet excelling in walk-forward refitting and corrected SARIMAX performing best in frozen regime.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current air-quality forecasting relies on complex, data-intensive models; this study investigates whether lightweight, interpretable approaches can provide competitive performance for practical urban air-quality prediction.

Method: Developed leakage-aware forecasting workflow with chronological data partitioning, preprocessing, feature selection, and exogenous-driver modeling under Perfect Prognosis setting. Evaluated three forecasting families (SARIMAX, Facebook Prophet, NeuralProphet) under two adaptive regimes: weekly walk-forward refitting and frozen forecasting with online residual correction.

Result: Facebook Prophet achieved strongest performance under walk-forward refitting (MAE 37.61, RMSE 50.10) with substantially less execution time than NeuralProphet. In frozen regime, online residual correction improved Facebook Prophet and SARIMAX, with corrected SARIMAX yielding lowest overall error (MAE 32.50, RMSE 46.85). NeuralProphet remained less accurate and unstable across regimes.

Conclusion: Lightweight additive forecasting strategies can remain highly competitive for urban air-quality prediction, offering practical balance between accuracy, interpretability, and computational efficiency, with corrected SARIMAX and Facebook Prophet showing particular promise.

Abstract: Accurate short-term air-quality forecasting is essential for public health protection and urban management, yet many recent forecasting frameworks rely on complex, data-intensive, and computationally demanding models. This study investigates whether lightweight and interpretable forecasting approaches can provide competitive performance for hourly PM2.5 prediction in Beijing, China. Using multi-year pollutant and meteorological time-series data, we developed a leakage-aware forecasting workflow that combined chronological data partitioning, preprocessing, feature selection, and exogenous-driver modeling under the Perfect Prognosis setting. Three forecasting families were evaluated: SARIMAX, Facebook Prophet, and NeuralProphet. To assess practical deployment behavior, the models were tested under two adaptive regimes: weekly walk-forward refitting and frozen forecasting with online residual correction. Results showed clear differences in both predictive accuracy and computational efficiency. Under walk-forward refitting, Facebook Prophet achieved the strongest completed performance, with an MAE of $37.61$ and an RMSE of $50.10$, while also requiring substantially less execution time than NeuralProphet. In the frozen-model regime, online residual correction improved Facebook Prophet and SARIMAX, with corrected SARIMAX yielding the lowest overall error (MAE $32.50$; RMSE $46.85$). NeuralProphet remained less accurate and less stable across both regimes, and residual correction did not improve its forecasts. Notably, corrected Facebook Prophet reached nearly the same error as its walk-forward counterpart while reducing runtime from $15$ min $21.91$ sec to $46.60$ sec. These findings show that lightweight additive forecasting strategies can remain highly competitive for urban air-quality prediction, offering a practical balance between accuracy, interpretability, ...

</details>


### [243] [Missing-Aware Multimodal Fusion for Unified Microservice Incident Management](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.25538)
*Wenzhuo Qian,Hailiang Zhao,Ziqi Wang,Zhipeng Gao,Jiayi Chen,Zhiwei Ling,Shuiguang Deng*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ARMOR is a robust self-supervised framework for microservice incident management that handles missing modalities through modality-specific encoding and missing-aware fusion, achieving SOTA performance even with severe data loss.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing unified frameworks for microservice incident management assume perfect multimodal data completeness, but in practice, network fluctuations and agent failures frequently cause missing modalities. Current approaches using static placeholders introduce imputation noise that masks anomalies and degrades performance.

Method: ARMOR features: (1) modality-specific asymmetric encoder that isolates distribution disparities among metrics, logs, and traces; (2) missing-aware gated fusion mechanism using learnable placeholders and dynamic bias compensation to prevent cross-modal interference from incomplete inputs; (3) self-supervised auto-regression with mask-guided reconstruction for joint optimization of anomaly detection, failure triage, and root cause localization.

Result: Extensive experiments demonstrate ARMOR achieves state-of-the-art performance under complete data conditions and maintains robust diagnostic accuracy even with severe modality loss. Anomaly detection and root cause localization require no fault labels, while failure triage relies only on failure-type annotations for the downstream classifier.

Conclusion: ARMOR provides a practical solution for real-world microservice incident management by addressing the critical challenge of missing modalities through robust self-supervised learning, enabling reliable automated diagnostics even with incomplete multimodal data.

Abstract: Automated incident management is critical for microservice reliability. While recent unified frameworks leverage multimodal data for joint optimization, they unrealistically assume perfect data completeness. In practice, network fluctuations and agent failures frequently cause missing modalities. Existing approaches relying on static placeholders introduce imputation noise that masks anomalies and degrades performance. To address this, we propose ARMOR, a robust self-supervised framework designed for missing modality scenarios. ARMOR features: (i) a modality-specific asymmetric encoder that isolates distribution disparities among metrics, logs, and traces; and (ii) a missing-aware gated fusion mechanism utilizing learnable placeholders and dynamic bias compensation to prevent cross-modal interference from incomplete inputs. By employing self-supervised auto-regression with mask-guided reconstruction, ARMOR jointly optimizes anomaly detection (AD), failure triage (FT), and root cause localization (RCL). AD and RCL require no fault labels, while FT relies solely on failure-type annotations for the downstream classifier. Extensive experiments demonstrate that ARMOR achieves state-of-the-art performance under complete data conditions and maintains robust diagnostic accuracy even with severe modality loss.

</details>


### [244] [An Integrative Genome-Scale Metabolic Modeling and Machine Learning Framework for Predicting and Optimizing Biofuel-Relevant Biomass Production in Saccharomyces cerevisiae](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.25561)
*Neha K. Nair,Aaron D'Souza*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A computational framework combining Yeast9 genome-scale metabolic model with machine learning and optimization techniques to predict and enhance biomass flux in Saccharomyces cerevisiae, achieving high prediction accuracy and significant flux improvements.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Accurately predicting biomass flux across diverse environmental and genetic perturbations remains a significant challenge for rational strain design in industrial biotechnology using Saccharomyces cerevisiae.

Method: Combined Yeast9 genome-scale metabolic model with machine learning (Random Forest, XGBoost, variational autoencoder, SHAP analysis) and optimization techniques (in silico overexpression, Bayesian optimization, generative adversarial network) to predict, interpret, and enhance biomass flux.

Result: Random Forest and XGBoost achieved R2 of 0.99989 and 0.9990 respectively; variational autoencoder revealed four distinct metabolic clusters; SHAP identified glycolysis, TCA cycle, and lipid biosynthesis as key determinants; in silico overexpression achieved 0.979 gDW/hr; Bayesian optimization produced 12-fold increase (0.0858 to 1.041 gDW/hr); GAN proposed stoichiometrically feasible novel flux configurations.

Conclusion: The framework demonstrates how genome-scale simulation, interpretable machine learning, and generative modeling can advance yeast metabolic engineering for industrial biotechnology applications.

Abstract: Saccharomyces cerevisiae is a cornerstone organism in industrial biotechnology, valued for its genetic tractability and robust fermentative capacity. Accurately predicting biomass flux across diverse environmental and genetic perturbations remains a significant challenge for rational strain design. We present a computational framework combining the Yeast9 genome-scale metabolic model with machine learning and optimization to predict, interpret, and enhance biomass flux. Flux balance analysis generated 2,000 flux profiles by varying glucose, oxygen, and ammonium uptake rates. Random Forest and XGBoost regressors achieved R2 of 0.99989 and 0.9990, respectively. A variational autoencoder revealed four distinct metabolic clusters, and SHAP analysis identified glycolysis, the TCA cycle, and lipid biosynthesis as key biomass determinants. In silico overexpression achieved a biomass flux of 0.979 gDW/hr, while Bayesian optimization of nutrient constraints produced a 12-fold increase (0.0858 to 1.041 gDW/hr). A generative adversarial network proposed stoichiometrically feasible novel flux configurations. This framework demonstrates how genome-scale simulation, interpretable ML, and generative modeling can advance yeast metabolic engineering.

</details>


### [245] [Revisiting On-Policy Distillation: Empirical Failure Modes and Simple Fixes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.25562)
*Yuqian Fu,Haohuan Huang,Kaiwen Jiang,Yuanheng Zhu,Dongbin Zhao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper analyzes on-policy distillation (OPD) for LLM post-training, identifies failure modes of sampled-token OPD in long-horizon settings, and proposes teacher top-K local support matching as a more stable alternative.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Sampled-token OPD is fragile in long-horizon settings because it reduces distribution matching to a one-token signal and becomes unreliable as student rollouts drift from teacher-common prefixes. The paper aims to address these limitations for more stable LLM post-training.

Method: Theoretical analysis of token-level OPD bias vs variance tradeoff, empirical identification of three failure modes, and proposal of teacher top-K local support matching implemented as truncated reverse-KL with top-p rollout sampling and special-token masking.

Result: The proposed objective yields more stable optimization and better downstream performance than sampled-token OPD across single-task math reasoning and multi-task agentic-plus-math training.

Conclusion: Teacher top-K local support matching addresses the limitations of sampled-token OPD, providing more reliable guidance and stable learning for LLM post-training in long-horizon settings.

Abstract: On-policy distillation (OPD) is appealing for large language model (LLM) post-training because it evaluates teacher feedback on student-generated rollouts rather than fixed teacher traces. In long-horizon settings, however, the common sampled-token variant is fragile: it reduces distribution matching to a one-token signal and becomes increasingly unreliable as rollouts drift away from prefixes the teacher commonly visits. We revisit OPD from the estimator and implementation sides. Theoretically, token-level OPD is biased relative to sequence-level reverse-KL, but it has a much tighter worst-case variance bound; our toy study shows the same tradeoff empirically, with stronger future-reward coupling producing higher gradient variance and less stable learning. Empirically, we identify three failure modes of sampled-token OPD: an imbalanced one-token signal, unreliable teacher guidance on student-generated prefixes, and distortions caused by tokenizer or special-token mismatch. We address these issues with teacher top-K local support matching, implemented as truncated reverse-KL with top-p rollout sampling and special-token masking. Across single-task math reasoning and multi-task agentic-plus-math training, this objective yields more stable optimization and better downstream performance than sampled-token OPD.

</details>


### [246] [Spatiotemporal System Forecasting with Irregular Time Steps via Masked Autoencoder](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.25597)
*Kewei Zhu,Yanze Xin,Jinwei Hu,Xiaoyuan Cheng,Yiming Yang,Sibo Cheng*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A Physics-Spatiotemporal Masked Autoencoder method for predicting high-dimensional dynamical systems with irregular time steps, achieving improved accuracy and efficiency without data imputation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current data-driven algorithms struggle with predicting high-dimensional dynamical systems that have irregular time steps, which arise from missing data, sparse observations, or adaptive computational techniques, leading to reduced prediction accuracy.

Method: A novel Physics-Spatiotemporal Masked Autoencoder that integrates convolutional autoencoders for spatial feature extraction with masked autoencoders optimized for irregular time series, using attention mechanisms to reconstruct entire physical sequences in a single prediction pass without data imputation.

Result: The method demonstrates significant improvements in prediction accuracy, robustness to nonlinearities, and computational efficiency over traditional convolutional and recurrent network methods on multiple simulated datasets and real-world ocean temperature data.

Conclusion: The model effectively captures complex spatiotemporal patterns without requiring domain-specific knowledge, with potential applications in climate modelling, fluid dynamics, ocean forecasting, environmental monitoring, and scientific computing.

Abstract: Predicting high-dimensional dynamical systems with irregular time steps presents significant challenges for current data-driven algorithms. These irregularities arise from missing data, sparse observations, or adaptive computational techniques, reducing prediction accuracy. To address these limitations, we propose a novel method: a Physics-Spatiotemporal Masked Autoencoder. This method integrates convolutional autoencoders for spatial feature extraction with masked autoencoders optimised for irregular time series, leveraging attention mechanisms to reconstruct the entire physical sequence in a single prediction pass. The model avoids the need for data imputation while preserving physical integrity of the system. Here, 'physics' refers to high-dimensional fields generated by underlying dynamical systems, rather than the enforcement of explicit physical constraints or PDE residuals. We evaluate this approach on multiple simulated datasets and real-world ocean temperature data. The results demonstrate that our method achieves significant improvements in prediction accuracy, robustness to nonlinearities, and computational efficiency over traditional convolutional and recurrent network methods. The model shows potential for capturing complex spatiotemporal patterns without requiring domain-specific knowledge, with applications in climate modelling, fluid dynamics, ocean forecasting, environmental monitoring, and scientific computing.

</details>


### [247] [Anchored-Branched Steady-state WInd Flow Transformer (AB-SWIFT): a metamodel for 3D atmospheric flow in urban environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.25635)
*Armand de Villeroché,Rem-Sophia Mouradi,Vincent Le Guen,Sibo Cheng,Marc Bocquet,Alban Farchi,Patrick Armand,Patrick Massin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: AB-SWIFT is a transformer-based deep learning model with branched architecture for urban air flow prediction, outperforming state-of-the-art models on atmospheric simulations with varying urban geometries and stratifications.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional CFD simulations for urban air flow are computationally expensive, while existing deep learning models struggle with high variations in urban geometry and large mesh sizes, necessitating a more robust surrogate model.

Method: Proposes AB-SWIFT (Anchored Branched Steady-state Wind Flow Transformer), a transformer-based model with internal branched structure specifically designed for atmospheric flow modeling, trained on a database of atmospheric simulations with randomized urban geometries and mixed atmospheric stratifications.

Result: AB-SWIFT achieves the best accuracy on all predicted fields compared to state-of-the-art transformers and graph-based models, demonstrating superior performance in urban air flow prediction.

Conclusion: The proposed AB-SWIFT model effectively addresses challenges in urban air flow modeling by combining transformer architecture with branched structure, offering an accurate and efficient alternative to costly CFD computations.

Abstract: Air flow modeling at a local scale is essential for applications such as pollutant dispersion modeling or wind farm modeling. To circumvent costly Computational Fluid Dynamics (CFD) computations, deep learning surrogate models have recently emerged as promising alternatives. However, in the context of urban air flow, deep learning models struggle to adapt to the high variations of the urban geometry and to large mesh sizes. To tackle these challenges, we introduce Anchored Branched Steady-state WInd Flow Transformer (AB-SWIFT), a transformer-based model with an internal branched structure uniquely designed for atmospheric flow modeling. We train our model on a specially designed database of atmospheric simulations around randomised urban geometries and with a mixture of unstable, neutral, and stable atmospheric stratifications. Our model reaches the best accuracy on all predicted fields compared to state-of-the-art transformers and graph-based models. Our code and data is available at https://github.com/cerea-daml/abswift.

</details>


### [248] [Uncertainty-Guided Label Rebalancing for CPS Safety Monitoring](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.25670)
*John Ayotunde,Qinghua Xu,Guancheng Wang,Lionel C. Briand*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: U-Balance: A supervised approach that leverages behavioral uncertainty to rebalance imbalanced CPS safety monitoring datasets by probabilistically relabeling safe windows with high uncertainty as unsafe, outperforming baselines by 14.3 percentage points in F1 score.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Safety monitoring in Cyber-Physical Systems suffers from extreme class imbalance (rare unsafe events), degrading predictor performance. Standard rebalancing techniques fail on time-series CPS telemetry, while behavioral uncertainty (correlated with safety outcomes) remains unexplored for safety monitoring.

Method: U-Balance trains a GatedMLP-based uncertainty predictor that converts telemetry windows into distributional kinematic features and outputs uncertainty scores. It then applies uncertainty-guided label rebalancing (uLNR) that probabilistically relabels safe-labeled windows with high uncertainty as unsafe, enriching the minority class without synthetic data generation. Finally, a safety predictor is trained on the rebalanced dataset.

Result: Evaluation on a large-scale UAV benchmark with 46:1 safe-to-unsafe ratio shows moderate but significant correlation between behavioral uncertainty and safety. uLNR is identified as the most effective strategy to exploit uncertainty information. U-Balance achieves 0.806 F1 score, outperforming the strongest baseline by 14.3 percentage points while maintaining competitive inference efficiency.

Conclusion: U-Balance effectively addresses extreme class imbalance in CPS safety monitoring by leveraging behavioral uncertainty through uncertainty-guided label rebalancing, significantly improving safety prediction performance without generating unrealistic synthetic samples.

Abstract: Safety monitoring is essential for Cyber-Physical Systems (CPSs). However, unsafe events are rare in real-world CPS operations, creating an extreme class imbalance that degrades safety predictors. Standard rebalancing techniques perform poorly on time-series CPS telemetry, either generating unrealistic synthetic samples or overfitting on the minority class. Meanwhile, behavioral uncertainty in CPS operations, defined as the degree of doubt or uncertainty in CPS decisions , is often correlated with safety outcomes but unexplored in safety monitoring. To that end, we propose U-Balance, a supervised approach that leverages behavioral uncertainty to rebalance imbalanced datasets prior to training a safety predictor. U-Balance first trains a GatedMLP-based uncertainty predictor that summarizes each telemetry window into distributional kinematic features and outputs an uncertainty score. It then applies an uncertainty-guided label rebalancing (uLNR) mechanism that probabilistically relabels \textit{safe}-labeled windows with unusually high uncertainty as \textit{unsafe}, thereby enriching the minority class with informative boundary samples without synthesizing new data. Finally, a safety predictor is trained on the rebalanced dataset for safety monitoring. We evaluate U-Balance on a large-scale UAV benchmark with a 46:1 safe-to-unsafe ratio. Results confirm a moderate but significant correlation between behavioral uncertainty and safety. We then identify uLNR as the most effective strategy to exploit uncertainty information, compared to direct early and late fusion. U-Balance achieves a 0.806 F1 score, outperforming the strongest baseline by 14.3 percentage points, while maintaining competitive inference efficiency. Ablation studies confirm that both the GatedMLP-based uncertainty predictor and the uLNR mechanism contribute significantly to U-Balance's effectiveness.

</details>


### [249] [Longitudinal Digital Phenotyping for Early Cognitive-Motor Screening](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.25673)
*Diego Jimenez-Oviedo,Ruben Vera-Rodriguez,Ruben Tolosana,Juan Carlos Ruiz-Garcia,Jaime Herreros-Rodriguez*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: AI-driven longitudinal framework using tablet interactions identifies three distinct cognitive-motor developmental profiles (low, medium, high performance) with high stability in low-performance cluster, enabling early screening and personalized interventions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional assessments for atypical cognitive-motor development rely on subjective, static evaluations, creating a need for continuous, objective monitoring through digital biomarkers for early detection and timely intervention.

Method: AI-driven longitudinal framework analyzing tablet-based interactions from children aged 18 months to 8 years across six cognitive-motor tasks, using dimensionality reduction (t-SNE) and unsupervised clustering (K-Means++) to identify developmental phenotypes and track individual transitions over time.

Result: Identified three distinct developmental profiles: low, medium, and high performance. Longitudinal tracking revealed high stability (>90% retention) in the low-performance cluster during early years, suggesting persistence of early deficits without intervention, while higher-performance clusters showed greater variability potentially reflecting engagement factors.

Conclusion: Validates unsupervised learning on touchscreen data for uncovering heterogeneous developmental paths, with identified profiles serving as scalable, data-driven proxies for cognitive growth that can form the foundation for early screening tools and personalized pediatric interventions.

Abstract: Early detection of atypical cognitive-motor development is critical for timely intervention, yet traditional assessments rely heavily on subjective, static evaluations. The integration of digital devices offers an opportunity for continuous, objective monitoring through digital biomarkers. In this work, we propose an AI-driven longitudinal framework to model developmental trajectories in children aged 18 months to 8 years. Using a dataset of tablet-based interactions collected over multiple academic years, we analyzed six cognitive-motor tasks (e.g., fine motor control, reaction time). We applied dimensionality reduction (t-SNE) and unsupervised clustering (K-Means++) to identify distinct developmental phenotypes and tracked individual transitions between these profiles over time. Our analysis reveals three distinct profiles: low, medium, and high performance. Crucially, longitudinal tracking highlights a high stability in the low-performance cluster (>90% retention in early years), suggesting that early deficits tend to persist without intervention. Conversely, higher-performance clusters show greater variability, potentially reflecting engagement factors. This study validates the use of unsupervised learning on touchscreen data to uncover heterogeneous developmental paths. The identified profiles serve as scalable, data-driven proxies for cognitive growth, offering a foundation for early screening tools and personalized pediatric interventions.

</details>


### [250] [On Neural Scaling Laws for Weather Emulation through Continual Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.25687)
*Shashank Subramanian,Alexander Kiefer,Arnur Nigmetov,Amir Gholami,Dmitriy Morozov,Michael W. Mahoney*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Neural scaling laws for weather forecasting models show predictable performance trends with compute-optimal training regimes, outperforming standard schedules with continual training and cooldown phases.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To study neural scaling laws in Scientific Machine Learning, specifically for weather forecasting, as these scaling laws are fundamental for building foundation models in NLP and CV but less explored in scientific domains.

Method: Adopted minimal, scalable Swin Transformer architecture with continual training using constant learning rates and periodic cooldowns. Systematically explored model and dataset sizes under various compute budgets to construct IsoFLOP curves and identify compute-optimal regimes.

Result: Models trained with minimalist approach follow predictable scaling trends and outperform standard cosine learning rate schedules. Cooldown phases improve downstream performance for multi-step rollouts and sharper predictions. Extrapolation to larger scales reveals potential performance limits.

Conclusion: Neural scaling laws serve as important diagnostic tools for efficient resource allocation in scientific ML, with compute-optimal training regimes identified for weather forecasting models.

Abstract: Neural scaling laws, which in some domains can predict the performance of large neural networks as a function of model, data, and compute scale, are the cornerstone of building foundation models in Natural Language Processing and Computer Vision. We study neural scaling in Scientific Machine Learning, focusing on models for weather forecasting. To analyze scaling behavior in as simple a setting as possible, we adopt a minimal, scalable, general-purpose Swin Transformer architecture, and we use continual training with constant learning rates and periodic cooldowns as an efficient training strategy. We show that models trained in this minimalist way follow predictable scaling trends and even outperform standard cosine learning rate schedules. Cooldown phases can be re-purposed to improve downstream performance, e.g., enabling accurate multi-step rollouts over longer forecast horizons as well as sharper predictions through spectral loss adjustments. We also systematically explore a wide range of model and dataset sizes under various compute budgets to construct IsoFLOP curves, and we identify compute-optimal training regimes. Extrapolating these trends to larger scales highlights potential performance limits, demonstrating that neural scaling can serve as an important diagnostic for efficient resource allocation. We open-source our code for reproducibility.

</details>


### [251] [A Unified Memory Perspective for Probabilistic Trustworthy AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.25692)
*Xueji Zhao,Likai Pei,Jianbo Liu,Kai Ni,Ningyuan Cao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper presents a unified framework treating deterministic memory access as a special case of stochastic sampling, revealing that increasing stochastic demands reduce effective data-access efficiency and can drive systems into entropy-limited operation, with analysis of emerging probabilistic compute-in-memory architectures for trustworthy AI.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Trustworthy AI increasingly relies on probabilistic computation for robustness, interpretability, security and privacy, creating workloads that interleave deterministic data access with repeated stochastic sampling, shifting performance bottlenecks from arithmetic units to memory systems that must deliver both data and randomness.

Method: The authors present a unified data-access perspective where deterministic access is treated as a limiting case of stochastic sampling, enabling both modes to be analyzed within a common framework. They define memory-level evaluation criteria including unified operation, distribution programmability, efficiency, robustness to hardware non-idealities, and parallel compatibility.

Result: The unified framework reveals that increasing stochastic demand reduces effective data-access efficiency and can drive systems into entropy-limited operation. Using the defined criteria, the authors analyze limitations of conventional architectures and examine emerging probabilistic compute-in-memory approaches that integrate sampling with memory access.

Conclusion: The paper outlines pathways toward scalable hardware for trustworthy AI by analyzing emerging probabilistic compute-in-memory approaches that integrate sampling with memory access, addressing the fundamental shift in performance bottlenecks from arithmetic units to memory systems in probabilistic AI workloads.

Abstract: Trustworthy artificial intelligence increasingly relies on probabilistic computation to achieve robustness, interpretability, security and privacy. In practical systems, such workloads interleave deterministic data access with repeated stochastic sampling across models, data paths and system functions, shifting performance bottlenecks from arithmetic units to memory systems that must deliver both data and randomness. Here we present a unified data-access perspective in which deterministic access is treated as a limiting case of stochastic sampling, enabling both modes to be analyzed within a common framework. This view reveals that increasing stochastic demand reduces effective data-access efficiency and can drive systems into entropy-limited operation. Based on this insight, we define memory-level evaluation criteria, including unified operation, distribution programmability, efficiency, robustness to hardware non-idealities and parallel compatibility. Using these criteria, we analyze limitations of conventional architectures and examine emerging probabilistic compute-in-memory approaches that integrate sampling with memory access, outlining pathways toward scalable hardware for trustworthy AI.

</details>


### [252] [Neural Network Conversion of Machine Learning Pipelines](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.25699)
*Man-Ling Sung,Jan Silovsky,Man-Hung Siu,Herbert Gish,Chinnu Pittapally*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Transfer learning from non-neural ML pipelines (random forest teachers) to neural network students enables joint optimization and unified inference across multiple ML tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable joint optimization of various pipeline components and create a single unified inference engine for multiple ML tasks by transferring knowledge from traditional ML models to neural networks.

Method: Extends student-teacher learning to transfer from random forest classifiers (non-neural teachers) to neural network students, experimenting with various NN topologies on 100 OpenML tasks and using random forest for hyper-parameter selection.

Result: For majority of tasks, student neural networks can successfully mimic random forest teacher performance when appropriate NN hyper-parameters are selected.

Conclusion: Knowledge distillation from traditional ML models to neural networks is feasible and enables unified optimization, with random forest showing promise for hyper-parameter selection in this transfer learning context.

Abstract: Transfer learning and knowledge distillation has recently gained a lot of attention in the deep learning community. One transfer approach, the student-teacher learning, has been shown to successfully create ``small'' student neural networks that mimic the performance of a much bigger and more complex ``teacher'' networks. In this paper, we investigate an extension to this approach and transfer from a non-neural-based machine learning pipeline as teacher to a neural network (NN) student, which would allow for joint optimization of the various pipeline components and a single unified inference engine for multiple ML tasks. In particular, we explore replacing the random forest classifier by transfer learning to a student NN. We experimented with various NN topologies on 100 OpenML tasks in which random forest has been one of the best solutions. Our results show that for the majority of the tasks, the student NN can indeed mimic the teacher if one can select the right NN hyper-parameters. We also investigated the use of random forest for selecting the right NN hyper-parameters.

</details>


<div id='cs.IR'></div>

# cs.IR [[Back]](#toc)

### [253] [Pseudo Label NCF for Sparse OHC Recommendation: Dual Representation Learning and the Separability Accuracy Trade off](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.24750)
*Pronob Kumar Barman,Tera L. Reynolds. James Foulds*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: Pseudo Label NCF improves recommendation for online health communities under extreme interaction sparsity by using survey-derived pseudo labels to enhance neural collaborative filtering models, achieving better ranking performance and more interpretable embeddings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Online health communities face discovery challenges due to extreme interaction sparsity where users have minimal prior interactions, making traditional personalization difficult. The paper addresses cold-start recommendation problems in survey-driven settings where users provide intake vectors and support groups have structured feature profiles.

Method: Extends Neural Collaborative Filtering (NCF) architectures (Matrix Factorization, Multi-Layer Perceptron, NeuMF) with auxiliary pseudo label objective derived from survey group feature alignment using cosine similarity mapped to [0,1]. Creates Pseudo Label NCF with dual embedding spaces: main embeddings for ranking and pseudo label embeddings for semantic alignment.

Result: All pseudo label variants improve ranking performance on dataset of 165 users and 498 support groups using leave-one-out protocol: MLP improves HR@5 from 2.65% to 5.30%, NeuMF from 4.46% to 5.18%, and MF from 4.58% to 5.42%. Pseudo label embeddings show higher cosine silhouette scores (MF: 0.0394→0.0684, NeuMF: 0.0263→0.0653). Negative correlation observed between embedding separability and ranking accuracy.

Conclusion: Survey-derived pseudo labels effectively improve recommendation under extreme sparsity while producing interpretable task-specific embedding spaces, though there's a trade-off between interpretability (embedding separability) and ranking performance.

Abstract: Online Health Communities connect patients for peer support, but users face a discovery challenge when they have minimal prior interactions to guide personalization. We study recommendation under extreme interaction sparsity in a survey driven setting where each user provides a 16 dimensional intake vector and each support group has a structured feature profile. We extend Neural Collaborative Filtering architectures, including Matrix Factorization, Multi Layer Perceptron, and NeuMF, with an auxiliary pseudo label objective derived from survey group feature alignment using cosine similarity mapped to [0, 1]. The resulting Pseudo Label NCF learns dual embedding spaces: main embeddings for ranking and pseudo label embeddings for semantic alignment.
  We evaluate on a dataset of 165 users and 498 support groups using a leave one out protocol that reflects cold start conditions. All pseudo label variants improve ranking performance: MLP improves HR@5 from 2.65% to 5.30%, NeuMF from 4.46% to 5.18%, and MF from 4.58% to 5.42%. Pseudo label embedding spaces also show higher cosine silhouette scores than baseline embeddings, with MF improving from 0.0394 to 0.0684 and NeuMF from 0.0263 to 0.0653. We further observe a negative correlation between embedding separability and ranking accuracy, indicating a trade off between interpretability and performance.
  These results show that survey derived pseudo labels improve recommendation under extreme sparsity while producing interpretable task specific embedding spaces.

</details>


### [254] [Enhancing Online Support Group Formation Using Topic Modeling Techniques](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.24765)
*Pronob Kumar Barman,Tera L. Reynolds,James Foulds*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: Proposes two ML models (gDMR and gSTM) for automated support group formation in online health communities using text, demographics, and network data, outperforming baselines on MedHelp.org data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional support group formation in online health communities faces scalability issues, static categorization, and insufficient personalization, limiting effective peer support delivery.

Method: Developed two novel ML models: Group-specific Dirichlet Multinomial Regression (gDMR) integrates text, demographics, and network node embeddings; Group-specific Structured Topic Model (gSTM) adds sparsity constraints for distinct thematic groups.

Result: Both models substantially outperform LDA, DMR, and STM baselines on MedHelp.org dataset (2M+ posts) in predictive accuracy (held-out log likelihood), semantic coherence (UMass metric), and internal group consistency. gDMR provides practical covariates for implementation, while gSTM generates more distinct thematic groups.

Conclusion: The automated frameworks reduce reliance on manual curation, provide scalable solutions for personalized support group formation, and enhance peer interactions in OHCs with implications for patient engagement, community resilience, and health outcomes.

Abstract: Online health communities (OHCs) are vital for fostering peer support and improving health outcomes. Support groups within these platforms can provide more personalized and cohesive peer support, yet traditional support group formation methods face challenges related to scalability, static categorization, and insufficient personalization. To overcome these limitations, we propose two novel machine learning models for automated support group formation: the Group specific Dirichlet Multinomial Regression (gDMR) and the Group specific Structured Topic Model (gSTM). These models integrate user generated textual content, demographic profiles, and interaction data represented through node embeddings derived from user networks to systematically automate personalized, semantically coherent support group formation.
  We evaluate the models on a large scale dataset from MedHelp.org, comprising over 2 million user posts. Both models substantially outperform baseline methods including LDA, DMR, and STM in predictive accuracy (held out log likelihood), semantic coherence (UMass metric), and internal group consistency. The gDMR model yields group covariates that facilitate practical implementation by leveraging relational patterns from network structures and demographic data. In contrast, gSTM emphasizes sparsity constraints to generate more distinct and thematically specific groups. Qualitative analysis further validates the alignment between model generated groups and manually coded themes, showing the practical relevance of the models in informing groups that address diverse health concerns such as chronic illness management, diagnostic uncertainty, and mental health. By reducing reliance on manual curation, these frameworks provide scalable solutions that enhance peer interactions within OHCs, with implications for patient engagement, community resilience, and health outcomes.

</details>


### [255] [DIET: Learning to Distill Dataset Continually for Recommender Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.24958)
*Jiaqing Zhang,Hao Wang,Mingjia Yin,Bo Chen,Qinglin Jia,Rui Zhou,Ruiming Tang,ChaoYi Ma,Enhong Chen*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: DIET is a streaming dataset distillation framework for recommender systems that maintains a compact, evolving distilled dataset from streaming behavioral logs, enabling efficient model iteration with 1-2% data compression while preserving full-data training performance trends.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Retraining deep recommender models on full historical streaming data is prohibitively expensive for large-scale platforms, severely slowing down model development. There's a need for data-efficient approaches that can approximate full-data training behavior without repeatedly processing the entire evolving data stream.

Method: DIET formulates streaming dataset distillation for recommender systems, maintaining a compact distilled dataset that evolves alongside streaming data. Unlike static distillation methods, DIET models distilled data as evolving training memory updated stage-wise to align with long-term training dynamics. It uses principled initialization from influential samples and selective updates guided by influence-aware memory addressing within a bi-level optimization framework.

Result: DIET compresses training data to 1-2% of original size while preserving performance trends consistent with full-data training, reducing model iteration cost by up to 60×. The distilled datasets generalize well across different model architectures.

Conclusion: Streaming dataset distillation with DIET provides a scalable and reusable data foundation for recommender system development, enabling efficient model iteration while maintaining training fidelity across architectures.

Abstract: Modern deep recommender models are trained under a continual learning paradigm, relying on massive and continuously growing streaming behavioral logs. In large-scale platforms, retraining models on full historical data for architecture comparison or iteration is prohibitively expensive, severely slowing down model development. This challenge calls for data-efficient approaches that can faithfully approximate full-data training behavior without repeatedly processing the entire evolving data stream. We formulate this problem as \emph{streaming dataset distillation for recommender systems} and propose \textbf{DIET}, a unified framework that maintains a compact distilled dataset which evolves alongside streaming data while preserving training-critical signals. Unlike existing dataset distillation methods that construct a static distilled set, DIET models distilled data as an evolving training memory and updates it in a stage-wise manner to remain aligned with long-term training dynamics. DIET enables effective continual distillation through principled initialization from influential samples and selective updates guided by influence-aware memory addressing within a bi-level optimization framework. Experiments on large-scale recommendation benchmarks demonstrate that DIET compresses training data to as little as \textbf{1-2\%} of the original size while preserving performance trends consistent with full-data training, reducing model iteration cost by up to \textbf{60$\times$}. Moreover, the distilled datasets produced by DIET generalize well across different model architectures, highlighting streaming dataset distillation as a scalable and reusable data foundation for recommender system development.

</details>


### [256] [Unbiased Multimodal Reranking for Long-Tail Short-Video Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.24975)
*Wenyi Xu,Feiran Zhu,Songyang Li,Renzhe Zhou,Chao Zhang,Chenglei Dai,Yuren Mao,Yunjun Gao,Yi Zhang*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: LLM-driven multimodal reranking framework improves short-video search quality by addressing Matthew effect on long-tail queries, using LLM world knowledge to assess content quality without relying on sparse user behavior data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Short-video search suffers from severe Matthew effect on long-tail queries where sparse user behavior data causes models to amplify low-quality content like clickbait and shallow content. LLMs offer a solution with their inherent world knowledge for quality assessment independent of user interactions.

Method: Proposes a LLM-driven multimodal reranking framework with two-stage training: 1) uses multimodal evidence to construct high-quality annotations for supervised fine-tuning, 2) incorporates pairwise preference optimization to learn partial orderings among candidates. At inference, experience scores promote high-quality underexposed videos in reranking and guide page-level optimization via reinforcement learning.

Result: Method achieves consistent improvements over strong baselines in offline metrics (AUC, NDCG@K, human preference judgement). Online A/B test covering 15% traffic demonstrates gains in both user experience and consumption metrics.

Conclusion: The LLM-driven multimodal reranking framework effectively addresses the Matthew effect in long-tail video search scenarios, providing practical value by improving content quality assessment without relying on sparse user behavior data.

Abstract: Kuaishou serving hundreds of millions of searches daily, the quality of short-video search is paramount. However, it suffers from a severe Matthew effect on long-tail queries: sparse user behavior data causes models to amplify low-quality content such as clickbait and shallow content. The recent advancements in Large Language Models (LLMs) offer a new paradigm, as their inherent world knowledge provides a powerful mechanism to assess content quality, agnostic to sparse user interactions. To this end, we propose a LLM-driven multimodal reranking framework, which estimates user experience without real user behavior. The approach involves a two-stage training process: the first stage uses multimodal evidence to construct high-quality annotations for supervised fine-tuning, while the second stage incorporates pairwise preference optimization to help the model learn partial orderings among candidates. At inference time, the resulting experience scores are used to promote high-quality but underexposed videos in reranking, and further guide page-level optimization through reinforcement learning. Experiments show that the proposed method achieves consistent improvements over strong baselines in offline metrics including AUC, NDCG@K, and human preference judgement. An online A/B test covering 15\% of traffic further demonstrates gains in both user experience and consumption metrics, confirming the practical value of the approach in long-tail video search scenarios.

</details>


### [257] [Sparton: Fast and Memory-Efficient Triton Kernel for Learned Sparse Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.25011)
*Thong Nguyen,Cosimo Rulli,Franco Maria Nardini,Rossano Venturini,Andrew Yates*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: Sparton is a fused GPU kernel for Learned Sparse Retrieval models that eliminates the memory bottleneck of LM heads by performing early online reduction on logit tiles, achieving significant speedups and memory savings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LSR models like Splade use LM heads that create massive memory bottlenecks due to large vocabulary sizes (30k-250k tokens), materializing full logit matrices that limit model scaling and throttle throughput.

Method: Sparton is a Triton kernel that fuses tiled matrix multiplication, ReLU, Log1P, and max-reduction into a single GPU kernel, performing early online reduction directly on raw logit tiles to avoid materializing the full logit matrix.

Result: Sparton achieves up to 4.8x speedup and order-of-magnitude memory reduction vs PyTorch baselines. Integrated into Splade: 33% larger batch size, 14% faster training (30k vocab); 26x larger batch size, 2.5x faster training (250k vocab).

Conclusion: Sparton effectively eliminates the memory bottleneck in LSR models, enabling larger batch sizes and faster training without effectiveness loss, particularly beneficial for models with large vocabularies.

Abstract: State-of-the-art Learned Sparse Retrieval (LSR) models, such as Splade, typically employ a Language Modeling (LM) head to project latent hidden states into a lexically-anchored logit matrix. This intermediate matrix is subsequently transformed into a sparse lexical representation through element-wise operations (ReLU, Log1P) and max-pooling over the sequence dimension. Despite its effectiveness, the LM head creates a massive memory bottleneck due to the sheer size of the vocabulary (V), which can range from 30,000 to over 250,000 tokens in recent models. Materializing this matrix creates a significant memory bottleneck, limiting model scaling. The resulting I/O overhead between operators further throttles throughput and runtime performance. In this paper, we propose Sparton, a fast memory-efficient Triton kernel tailored for the LM head in LSR models. Sparton utilizes a fused approach that integrates the tiled matrix multiplication, ReLU, Log1P, and max-reduction into a single GPU kernel. By performing an early online reduction directly on raw logit tiles, Sparton avoids materializing the full logit matrix in memory. Our experiments demonstrate that the Sparton kernel, in isolation, achieves up to a 4.8x speedup and an order-of-magnitude reduction in peak memory usage compared to PyTorch baselines. Integrated into Splade (|V| ~ 30k), Sparton enables a 33% larger batch size and 14% faster training with no effectiveness loss. On a multilingual backbone (|V| ~ 250k), these gains jump to a 26x larger batch size and 2.5x faster training.

</details>


### [258] [Hyena Operator for Fast Sequential Recommendation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.25027)
*Jiahao Liu,Lin Li,Zhiyuan Li,Kaixi Hu,Kaize Shi,Jingling Yuan*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: HyenaRec is a novel sequential recommender that replaces quadratic-complexity attention with polynomial-based kernel parameterization and gated convolutions, achieving linear scaling with sequence length while maintaining or improving accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Attention-based sequential recommendation models suffer from quadratic complexity that makes long user histories prohibitively expensive. While sub-quadratic operators like Hyena exist for language modeling, they face challenges in recommendation due to limited representation capacity on sparse, long user sequences.

Method: HyenaRec integrates polynomial-based kernel parameterization with gated convolutions. It designs convolutional kernels using Legendre orthogonal polynomials for modeling long-term temporal dependencies, complemented by a gating mechanism to capture fine-grained short-term behavioral bursts, creating a hybrid architecture that balances global temporal evolution with localized user interests.

Result: HyenaRec consistently outperforms Attention-, Recurrent-, and other baselines in ranking accuracy across multiple real-world datasets. It trains significantly faster (up to 6x speedup) with particularly pronounced advantages on long-sequence scenarios, maintaining efficiency without sacrificing accuracy.

Conclusion: Polynomial-based kernel parameterization serves as a principled and scalable alternative to attention for sequential recommendation, offering linear scaling with sequence length while enhancing expressiveness for sparse, long user sequences.

Abstract: Sequential recommendation models, particularly those based on attention, achieve strong accuracy but incur quadratic complexity, making long user histories prohibitively expensive. Sub-quadratic operators such as Hyena provide efficient alternatives in language modeling, but their potential in recommendation remains underexplored. We argue that Hyena faces challenges in recommendation due to limited representation capacity on sparse, long user sequences. To address these challenges, we propose HyenaRec, a novel sequential recommender that integrates polynomial-based kernel parameterization with gated convolutions. Specifically, we design convolutional kernels using Legendre orthogonal polynomials, which provides a smooth and compact basis for modeling long-term temporal dependencies. A complementary gating mechanism captures fine-grained short-term behavioral bursts, yielding a hybrid architecture that balances global temporal evolution with localized user interests under sparse feedback. This construction enhances expressiveness while scaling linearly with sequence length. Extensive experiments on multiple real-world datasets demonstrate that HyenaRec consistently outperforms Attention-, Recurrent-, and other baselines in ranking accuracy. Moreover, it trains significantly faster (up to 6x speedup), with particularly pronounced advantages on long-sequence scenarios where efficiency is maintained without sacrificing accuracy. These results highlight polynomial-based kernel parameterization as a principled and scalable alternative to attention for sequential recommendation.

</details>


### [259] [AuthorityBench: Benchmarking LLM Authority Perception for Reliable Retrieval-Augmented Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.25092)
*Zhihui Yao,Hengran Zhang,Keping Bi*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: AuthorityBench is a comprehensive benchmark for evaluating LLMs' ability to perceive information authority, showing that authority perception is distinct from semantic understanding and that authority-guided filtering improves RAG performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: RAG systems enhance LLMs with external knowledge but remain vulnerable to low-authority sources that can propagate misinformation. The authors investigate whether LLMs can perceive information authority - a capability extending beyond semantic understanding - to address reliability issues in knowledge retrieval.

Method: Introduce AuthorityBench with three datasets: DomainAuth (10K web domains with PageRank-based authority), EntityAuth (22K entities with popularity-based authority), and RAGAuth (120 queries with documents of varying authority for downstream evaluation). Evaluate five LLMs using three judging methods (PointJudge, PairJudge, ListJudge) across multiple output formats.

Result: ListJudge and PairJudge with PointScore output achieve strongest correlation with ground-truth authority, while ListJudge offers optimal cost-effectiveness. Incorporating webpage text consistently degrades judgment performance, suggesting authority is distinct from textual style. Authority-guided filtering in RAG experiments largely improves answer accuracy.

Conclusion: LLMs can perceive information authority, and this capability is distinct from semantic understanding. AuthorityBench provides a comprehensive evaluation framework, and authority-guided filtering significantly enhances RAG reliability, validating the practical importance of authority perception for trustworthy knowledge retrieval.

Abstract: Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) enhances Large Language Models (LLMs) with external knowledge but remains vulnerable to low-authority sources that can propagate misinformation. We investigate whether LLMs can perceive information authority - a capability extending beyond semantic understanding. To address this, we introduce AuthorityBench, a comprehensive benchmark for evaluating LLM authority perception comprising three datasets: DomainAuth (10K web domains with PageRank-based authority), EntityAuth (22K entities with popularity-based authority), and RAGAuth (120 queries with documents of varying authority for downstream evaluation). We evaluate five LLMs using three judging methods (PointJudge, PairJudge, ListJudge) across multiple output formats. Results show that ListJudge and PairJudge with PointScore output achieve the strongest correlation with ground-truth authority, while ListJudge offers optimal cost-effectiveness. Notably, incorporating webpage text consistently degrades judgment performance, suggesting authority is distinct from textual style. Downstream experiments on RAG demonstrate that authority-guided filtering largely improves answer accuracy, validating the practical importance of authority perception for reliable knowledge retrieval. Code and benchmark are available at: https://github.com/Trustworthy-Information-Access/AuthorityBench.

</details>


### [260] [MCLMR: A Model-Agnostic Causal Learning Framework for Multi-Behavior Recommendation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.25126)
*Ranxu Zhang,Junjie Meng,Ying Sun,Ziqi Xu,Bing Yin,Hao Li,Yanyong Zhang,Chao Wang*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: MCLMR is a model-agnostic causal learning framework for multi-behavior recommendation that addresses confounding effects, dynamic behavior aggregation, and bias-aware representation alignment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multi-behavior recommendation methods lack principled frameworks to model complex confounding effects from user behavioral habits and item multi-behavior distributions, struggle with effective aggregation of heterogeneous auxiliary behaviors, and fail to align behavioral representations across semantic gaps while accounting for bias distortions.

Method: MCLMR constructs a causal graph to model confounding effects and performs interventions for unbiased preference estimation. It employs an Adaptive Aggregation module based on Mixture-of-Experts to dynamically fuse auxiliary behavior information and a Bias-aware Contrastive Learning module to align cross-behavior representations in a bias-aware manner.

Result: Extensive experiments on three real-world datasets demonstrate that MCLMR achieves significant performance improvements across various baseline models, validating its effectiveness and generality.

Conclusion: MCLMR provides a principled causal framework that addresses key limitations in multi-behavior recommendation, offering a model-agnostic solution that can be seamlessly integrated into various MBR architectures to improve recommendation performance.

Abstract: Multi-Behavior Recommendation (MBR) leverages multiple user interaction types (e.g., views, clicks, purchases) to enrich preference modeling and alleviate data sparsity issues in traditional single-behavior approaches. However, existing MBR methods face fundamental challenges: they lack principled frameworks to model complex confounding effects from user behavioral habits and item multi-behavior distributions, struggle with effective aggregation of heterogeneous auxiliary behaviors, and fail to align behavioral representations across semantic gaps while accounting for bias distortions. To address these limitations, we propose MCLMR, a novel model-agnostic causal learning framework that can be seamlessly integrated into various MBR architectures. MCLMR first constructs a causal graph to model confounding effects and performs interventions for unbiased preference estimation. Under this causal framework, it employs an Adaptive Aggregation module based on Mixture-of-Experts to dynamically fuse auxiliary behavior information and a Bias-aware Contrastive Learning module to align cross-behavior representations in a bias-aware manner. Extensive experiments on three real-world datasets demonstrate that MCLMR achieves significant performance improvements across various baseline models, validating its effectiveness and generality. All data and code will be made publicly available. For anonymous review, our code is available at the following the link: https://github.com/gitrxh/MCLMR.

</details>


### [261] [ColBERT-Att: Late-Interaction Meets Attention for Enhanced Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.25248)
*Raj Nath Patel,Sourav Dutta*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: ColBERT-Att enhances ColBERT's late interaction retrieval by integrating attention weights to better capture term importance, improving recall accuracy across multiple benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current late interaction models like ColBERT ignore attention weights that could capture term importance and provide better understanding of query-document relevance.

Method: Proposes ColBERT-Att, which explicitly integrates attention mechanism into ColBERT's late interaction framework to weight term similarities based on their importance.

Result: Shows improved recall accuracy on MS-MARCO, BEIR, and LoTTE benchmark datasets compared to baseline ColBERT.

Conclusion: Integrating attention weights into late interaction retrieval enhances performance by better capturing term importance for relevance assessment.

Abstract: Vector embeddings from pre-trained language models form a core component in Neural Information Retrieval systems across a multitude of knowledge extraction tasks. The paradigm of late interaction, introduced in ColBERT, demonstrates high accuracy along with runtime efficiency. However, the current formulation fails to take into account the attention weights of query and document terms, which intuitively capture the "importance" of similarities between them, that might lead to a better understanding of relevance between the queries and documents. This work proposes ColBERT-Att, to explicitly integrate attention mechanism into the late interaction framework for enhanced retrieval performance. Empirical evaluation of ColBERT-Att depicts improvements in recall accuracy on MS-MARCO as well as on a wide range of BEIR and LoTTE benchmark datasets.

</details>


### [262] [Supercharging Federated Intelligence Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.25374)
*Dimitris Stripelis,Patrick Foley,Mohammad Naseri,William Lindskog-Münzing,Chong Shen Ng,Daniel Janes Beutel,Nicholas D. Lane*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: Secure Federated RAG system using Flower framework with local silo retrieval, server-side aggregation in confidential compute environment, and cascading inference with third-party models while maintaining confidentiality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional RAG systems assume centralized document access, which fails when knowledge is distributed across private data silos, creating privacy and security challenges for cross-silo knowledge retrieval.

Method: Federated RAG system using Flower framework with local retrieval at each silo, server-side aggregation and text generation in attested confidential compute environment, plus cascading inference incorporating third-party models as auxiliary context without compromising confidentiality.

Result: Enables confidential remote LLM inference even with honest-but-curious or compromised servers, maintains privacy while leveraging distributed knowledge, and allows integration of external models without weakening security guarantees.

Conclusion: Proposed system addresses privacy-preserving cross-silo knowledge retrieval through federated architecture with confidential computing and cascading inference, enabling secure RAG applications across distributed private data sources.

Abstract: RAG typically assumes centralized access to documents, which breaks down when knowledge is distributed across private data silos. We propose a secure Federated RAG system built using Flower that performs local silo retrieval, while server-side aggregation and text generation run inside an attested, confidential compute environment, enabling confidential remote LLM inference even in the presence of honest-but-curious or compromised servers. We also propose a cascading inference approach that incorporates a non-confidential third-party model (e.g., Amazon Nova) as auxiliary context without weakening confidentiality.

</details>
